**Illinois** ([/ˌɪlɪˈnɔɪ/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c7/En-us-Illinois.ogg/En-us-Illinois.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/c/c7/En-us-Illinois.ogg/En-us-Illinois.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> [*IL-ih-NOY*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key)) is a [state](18618239.md) in the [Midwestern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States). It borders on [Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) to its northeast, the [Wabash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wabash_River) and [Ohio rivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River) to its south, and the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) generally to its west.[^cite_note-7] Of the fifty U.S. states, Illinois has the [fifth-largest gross domestic product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_GDP) (GDP), the [sixth-largest population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_population), and the [25th-most land area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area). Its capital city is [Springfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Illinois) in the center of the state, and the state's largest city is [Chicago](6886.md), located in northeastern Illinois.&#x20;

Present-day Illinois was inhabited by [Indigenous cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas#History) for thousands of years. The French were the first Europeans to arrive, settling near the Mississippi and [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_River) rivers in the 17th-century [Illinois Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Country), as part of their sprawling colony of [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France). A century later, the revolutionary war [Illinois campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_campaign) prefigured American involvement in the region. Following [U.S. independence in 1783](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\)), which made the Mississippi River the national boundary, American settlers began arriving from [Kentucky](16846.md) via the Ohio River. Illinois was soon part of the United States' oldest territory, the [Northwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territory), and in 1818, it achieved [statehood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union). The [Erie Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erie_Canal) brought increased commercial activity in the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes), and the invention of the self-scouring [steel plow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plough) by Illinoisan [John Deere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Deere_\(inventor\)) turned the state's rich [prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie) into some of the world's most productive and valuable farmland, attracting [immigrant](15051.md) farmers from [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_American), [Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_American), [Hungary](13275.md), and elsewhere.&#x20;

In the mid-19th century, the [Illinois and Michigan Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_and_Michigan_Canal) and a sprawling railroad network facilitated trade, commerce, and settlement, making the state a transportation hub for the nation, especially in Chicago, which became the world's fastest growing city by the late 19th century.[^cite_note-8] By 1900, the growth of industrial jobs in the northern cities and [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal) mining in the central and southern areas attracted immigrants from [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) and [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe). Illinois became one of America's most industrialized states and remains a major manufacturing center.[^cite_note-9] The [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) from the South established a large black community, particularly in Chicago, which became a leading cultural, economic, and population center; the [Chicago metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area) holds about 65% of the state's 12.8 million residents. Illinois, alongside [Texas](29810.md), is one of the most [tornadically active](37530.md) states in the U.S; in 2023 alone, Illinois produced 136 tornadoes, the highest state by a significant margin.[^cite_note-IllinoisHighestNumberTornadoes-10]&#x20;

Two [World Heritage Sites](44940.md) are in Illinois: the ancient [Cahokia Mounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahokia_Mounds), and part of the [Wright architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_20th-Century_Architecture_of_Frank_Lloyd_Wright) site. A wide variety of [protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Illinois) seek to conserve Illinois' natural and cultural resources. Major centers of learning include the [University of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago), [University of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois), and [Northwestern University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_University). Three [U.S. presidents](24113.md) have been elected while residents of Illinois: [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln), [Ulysses S. Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant), and [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama); additionally, [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) was born and raised in the state, though he served for [California](5407.md). Illinois honors Lincoln with its official state slogan *Land of Lincoln*.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] The state is the site of the [Abraham Lincoln Presidential Library and Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln_Presidential_Library_and_Museum) in Springfield and the home of the [Barack Obama Presidential Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_Presidential_Center) in Chicago.&#x20;

&#x20;The [economy of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Illinois) is highly diversified, with Chicago serving as a global city for the U.S. Midwest, major industrial and [agricultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_productivity) hubs in the north and center, and [natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resource) such as coal, timber, and petroleum in the south. Owing to its central U.S. location, the state is a major [transportation hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_hub): the [Port of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Chicago) has access to the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) through the Great Lakes and [Saint Lawrence Seaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_Seaway) and to the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md) from the Mississippi River via the [Illinois Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Waterway). Chicago has been the nation's railroad hub since the 1860s,[^cite_note-13] and its [O'Hare International Airport](248449.md) has been among the world's busiest airports for decades. Illinois has long been considered a [microcosm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macrocosm_and_microcosm) of the United States and a [bellwether](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellwether_\(politics\)) in American culture, exemplified by the phrase *[Will it play in Peoria?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_it_play_in_Peoria%3F)*.[^cite_note-so-14]

## Etymology

See also: [Illinois Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Confederation) and [List of counties in Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Illinois)

"Illinois" is the modern spelling for the early [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism) [missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary) and explorers' name for the [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Confederation) [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States), a name that was spelled in many different ways in the early records.[^cite_note-15]&#x20;

American scholars previously thought the name *Illinois* meant 'man' or 'men' in the [Miami-Illinois language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami-Illinois_language), with the original *iliniwek* transformed via French into Illinois.[^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17] This etymology is not supported by the Illinois language,[^cite_note-Costa_9–12-18] as the word for "man" is *ireniwa*, and plural of "man" is *ireniwaki*. The name *[Illiniwek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiniwek)* has also been said to mean 'tribe of superior men',[^cite_note-Costa_9–12-18] which is a [false etymology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_etymology). The name *Illinois* derives from the Miami-Illinois verb *irenwe·wa* 'he speaks the regular way'. This was taken into the [Ojibwe language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ojibwe_language), perhaps in the [Ottawa dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_dialect), and modified into *ilinwe·* (pluralized as *ilinwe·k*). The French borrowed these forms, spelling the /we/ ending as *-ois*, a [transliteration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transliteration) of that sound in the French of that time. The current spelling form, *Illinois*, began to appear in the early 1670s, when French colonists had settled in the western area. The Illinois's name for themselves, as attested in all three of the French missionary-period dictionaries of Illinois, was *[Inoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inoka)*, of unknown meaning and unrelated to the other terms.[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-Costa_9–12-18]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Illinois)

### Pre-European



[Mississippian copper plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippian_copper_plate) found at the Saddle Site in [Union County, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_County,_Illinois)

[American Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) of successive cultures lived along the waterways of the Illinois area for thousands of years before the arrival of Europeans. The [Koster Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koster_Site) has been excavated and demonstrates 7,000 years of continuous habitation. [Cahokia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahokia), the largest regional [chiefdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiefdom) and [urban center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_center) of the [pre-Columbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Columbian_era) [Mississippian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippian_culture), was located near present-day [Collinsville, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collinsville,_Illinois). They built an urban complex of more than 100 [platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platform_mound) and [burial mounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burial_mound), a 50-acre (20 ha) [plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza) larger than 35 football fields,[^cite_note-PAUKETATCAHOKIA-20] and a woodhenge of sacred cedar, all in a planned design expressing the culture's cosmology. [Monks Mound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monks_Mound), the center of the site, is the largest Pre-Columbian structure north of the [Valley of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley_of_Mexico). It is 100 ft (30 m) high, 951 ft (290 m) long, 836 ft (255 m) wide, and covers 13.8 acres (5.6 ha).[^cite_note-SKELE-21] It contains about 814,000 cu yd (622,000 m<sup>3</sup>) of earth.[^cite_note-SNOW2010-22] It was topped by a structure thought to have measured about 105 ft (32 m) in length and 48 ft (15 m) in width, covered an area 5,000 sq ft (460 m<sup>2</sup>), and been as much as 50 ft (15 m) high, making its peak 150 ft (46 m) above the level of the plaza. Archaeological recoveries from Cahokia—including elaborate ceramics, sculpted stonework, engraved copper and [mica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mica) sheets, and a 20,000-shell-bead burial blanket—demonstrate that the site was an advanced urban center supporting residential clusters, markets, and diverse craft specialization.[^cite_note-23]&#x20;

The civilization collapsed in the 15th century due to undetermined causes, although theories suggest resource depletion and chronic intertribal warfare. Historian Suzanne Austin Alchon highlights that violent trauma accounted for one-third of adult deaths at a central [Illinois River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_River) valley site.[^cite_note-24] The next major power in the region was the [Illinois Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Confederation) or Illini, a political alliance.[^cite_note-25] Around the time of European contact in 1673, the Illinois confederation had an estimated population of over 10,000 people.[^cite_note-museum.state.il.us-26] As the Illini declined during the [Beaver Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver_Wars) era, members of the [Algonquian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquian_languages)-speaking [Potawatomi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potawatomi), [Miami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami_people), [Sauk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauk_people), and other tribes including the [Meskwaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meskwaki), [Iowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa_people), [Kickapoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kickapoo), [Mascouten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mascouten), [Piankeshaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piankeshaw), [Shawnee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawnee), [Wea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wea), and [Ho-Chunk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho-Chunk) came into the area from the east and north around the Great Lakes.[^cite_note-Nelson-27][^cite_note-28]&#x20;

### European exploration and settlement prior to 1800

Main articles: [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France); [Louisiana (New France)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)); [Canada (New France)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_\(New_France\)); [Illinois Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Country); [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War); [Treaty of Paris (1763)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)); [Province of Quebec (1763–1791)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)); [Indian Reserve (1763)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Reserve_\(1763\)); [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War); [Western theater of the American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_theater_of_the_American_Revolutionary_War); [Illinois County, Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_County,_Virginia); [Treaty of Paris (1783)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\)); [Northwest Ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Ordinance); and [Northwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territory)



Illinois in 1718, approximate modern state area highlighted, from *Carte de la Louisiane et du cours du Mississipi* by [Guillaume de L'Isle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillaume_de_L%27Isle)

[^cite_note-29]

French explorers [Jacques Marquette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Marquette) and [Louis Jolliet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Jolliet) explored the [Illinois River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_River) in 1673. Marquette soon after founded a mission at the [Grand Village of the Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Village_of_the_Illinois) in [Illinois Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Country). In 1680, French explorers under [René-Robert Cavelier, Sieur de La Salle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9-Robert_Cavelier,_Sieur_de_La_Salle) and [Henri de Tonti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_de_Tonti) constructed a fort at the site of present-day [Peoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria,_Illinois), and in 1682, a fort atop [Starved Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starved_Rock) in today's Starved Rock State Park. French Empire [Canadiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadiens) came south to settle particularly along the Mississippi River, and Illinois was part of first [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France), and then of [La Louisiane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Louisiane) until 1763, when it passed to the British with their defeat of France in the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War). The small French settlements continued, although many French migrated west to [Ste. Genevieve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ste._Genevieve,_Missouri) and [St. Louis, Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis,_Missouri), to evade British rule.[^cite_note-Biles-30]&#x20;

British forces garrisoned a few isolated posts in Illinois; however, the region saw virtually no British or American colonization. This lack of settlement was a direct consequence of imperial decrees, which first designated the territory as indigenous lands west of the Appalachians and subsequently absorbed it into the [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)). In 1778, [George Rogers Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Rogers_Clark) claimed [Illinois County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_County) for [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Virginia). In a compromise, Virginia (and other states that made various claims) ceded the area to the new United States in the 1780s and it became part of the [Northwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territory), administered by the federal government and later organized as states.[^cite_note-Biles-30]&#x20;

### 19th century

Main articles: [Indiana Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Territory), [Organic act § List of organic acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_act#List_of_organic_acts), [Illinois Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Territory), [Admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union), and [List of U.S. states by date of admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union)

See also: [History of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Chicago) and [History of Nauvoo, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Nauvoo,_Illinois)

#### Prior to statehood



The bell donated by King Louis XV in 1741 to the French mission at Kaskaskia. It was later called the "Liberty Bell of the West", after it was rung to celebrate U.S. victory in the Revolution

The [Illinois-Wabash Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois-Wabash_Company) was an early claimant to much of Illinois. The [Illinois Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Territory) was created on February 3, 1809, with its capital at [Kaskaskia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaskaskia,_Illinois), an early French settlement.&#x20;

During the discussions leading up to Illinois's [admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union), the proposed northern boundary of the state was moved twice.[^cite_note-31] The original provisions of the [Northwest Ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Ordinance) had specified a boundary that would have been tangent to the southern tip of Lake Michigan. Such a boundary would have left Illinois with no shoreline on Lake Michigan at all. However, as Indiana had successfully been granted a 10 mi (16 km) northern extension of its boundary to provide it with a usable lakefront, the original bill for Illinois statehood, submitted to Congress on January 23, 1818, stipulated a northern border at the same latitude as Indiana's, which is defined as 10 miles north of the southernmost extremity of Lake Michigan. However, the Illinois delegate, [Nathaniel Pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Pope), wanted more, and lobbied to have the boundary moved further north. The final bill passed by Congress included an amendment to shift the border to 42° 30' north, which is approximately 51 mi (82 km) north of the Indiana northern border. This shift added 8,500 sq mi (22,000 km<sup>2</sup>) to the state, including the [lead mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead_mining) region near [Galena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galena,_Illinois). More importantly, it added nearly 50 miles of Lake Michigan shoreline and the Chicago River. Pope and others envisioned a canal that would connect the Chicago and Illinois rivers and thus connect the Great Lakes to the Mississippi.&#x20;

#### State of Illinois prior to the Civil War



In 1818, Illinois became the 21st U.S. state. The southern portion of [Illinois Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Territory) was admitted as the state of Illinois, and the rest was joined to [Michigan Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Territory).



The second [Governor of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Illinois), [Edward Coles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Coles) brought his slaves from his home state of [Virginia](32432.md) to give them their [freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manumission) when they arrived in Illinois.



[Old State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_State_Capitol_State_Historic_Site): Abraham Lincoln and other area legislators were instrumental in moving the state capitol to centrally located Springfield in 1839.

In 1818, Illinois became the 21st U.S. state. The capital remained at Kaskaskia, headquartered in a small building rented by the state. In 1819, [Vandalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandalia,_Illinois) became the capital, and over the next 18 years, three separate buildings were built to serve successively as the capitol building. In 1837, the state legislators representing [Sangamon County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangamon_County), under the leadership of state representative [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln), succeeded in having the capital moved to [Springfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Illinois),[^cite_note-32] where a [fifth capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_State_Capitol_State_Historic_Site_\(Illinois\)) building was constructed. A [sixth capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_State_Capitol) building was erected in 1867, which continues to serve as the Illinois capitol today.&#x20;

Though it was ostensibly a "[free state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_and_free_states)", there was nonetheless [slavery in Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_Illinois). French colonists introduced enslavement to the Illinois region in the 1720s, a practice expanded by American settlers migrating from [Kentucky](16846.md). Although the Northwest Ordinance of 1787 nominally banned slavery, the prohibition was not enforced against pre-existing slaveholders. Consequently, when Illinois achieved statehood in 1818 and transitioned out of the Ordinance's jurisdiction, approximately 900 people remained enslaved within the state. As the southern part of the state, later known as "Egypt" or "Little Egypt",[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34] was largely settled by migrants from the South, the section was hostile to free blacks. [Edward Coles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Coles), the second Governor of Illinois who was born in Virginia, participated in a campaign to block extending existing slavery in Illinois after winning the [1822 Illinois gubernatorial election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1822_Illinois_gubernatorial_election). In 1824, state residents voted against making slavery legal by a vote of 6640 against to 4972 for.[^cite_note-35]&#x20;

Still, most residents opposed allowing free blacks as permanent residents. Some settlers brought in slaves seasonally or as house servants.[^cite_note-36] The [Illinois Constitution of 1848](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Illinois) was written with a provision for exclusionary laws to be passed. In 1853, [John A. Logan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_A._Logan) helped pass a law to prohibit all African Americans, including [freedmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedman), from settling in the state.[^cite_note-37]&#x20;

The winter of 1830–1831 is called the "Winter of the Deep Snow";[^cite_note-38] a sudden, deep snowfall blanketed the state, making travel impossible for the rest of the winter, and many travelers perished. Several severe winters followed, including the "Winter of the Sudden Freeze". On December 20, 1836, a fast-moving cold front passed through, freezing puddles in minutes and killing many travelers who could not reach shelter. The adverse weather resulted in crop failures in the northern part of the state. The southern part of the state shipped food north, and this may have contributed to its name, "[Little Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois)", after the Biblical story of [Joseph in Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_\(Genesis\)) supplying grain to his brothers.[^cite_note-39]&#x20;

In 1832, the [Black Hawk War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hawk_War) was fought in Illinois and present-day [Wisconsin](33127.md) between the United States and a coalition of the [Sauk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sauk_people), [Meskwaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meskwaki), and [Kickapoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kickapoo) peoples, who had been forced to leave their homes and relocate to Iowa in 1831. The tribes had lost their territory east of the Mississippi river in Illinois under a [disputed treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_St._Louis_\(1804\)) in 1804. The natives, under Sauk chief [Black Hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Hawk_\(Sauk_leader\)), attempted to return to Illinois in April 1832 to reclaim this land. They were attacked and defeated by the [U.S. Militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militia_\(United_States\)) and rival tribes allied with the US forces, including the [Potawatomi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potawatomi), [Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakota_people), [Menominee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menominee), and [Ho-Chunk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ho-Chunk). The survivors of Black Hawk's band were forced back to Iowa.[^cite_note-40] This represented the end of native resistance to white settlement in the Chicago and Northern Illinois regions.[^cite_note-41] By 1832, when the last native lands in Illinois were ceded to the United States, the indigenous population of the state had been reduced by infectious diseases, warfare, and [forced westward removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removal) to only one village with fewer than 300 inhabitants.[^cite_note-museum.state.il.us-26]&#x20;

By 1839, the [Latter Day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latter_Day_Saints) had founded a [utopian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utopian) city called [Nauvoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nauvoo,_Illinois), formerly called Commerce. Located in [Hancock County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hancock_County,_Illinois) along the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River), Nauvoo flourished and, by 1844, briefly surpassed Chicago for the position of the state's largest city.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43] But in that same year, the [Latter Day Saint movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latter_Day_Saint_movement) founder, [Joseph Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Smith), [was killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Joseph_Smith) in the [Carthage Jail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage_Jail), about 30 miles away from Nauvoo. Following a [succession crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_crisis_\(Latter_Day_Saints\)), [Brigham Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigham_Young) led most Latter Day Saints out of Illinois in a [mass exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mormon_pioneers) to present-day [Utah](31716.md); after close to six years of rapid development, Nauvoo quickly declined afterward.&#x20;

After it was established in 1833, [Chicago](6886.md) gained prominence as a [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) port, and then as an [Illinois and Michigan Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_and_Michigan_Canal) port after 1848, and as a rail hub soon afterward. By 1857, Chicago was Illinois's largest city.[^cite_note-Biles-30] With the tremendous growth of mines and factories in the state in the 19th century, Illinois was the ground for the formation of [labor unions in the United States](2474406.md).&#x20;

In 1847, after lobbying by [Dorothea L. Dix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dorothea_Dix), Illinois became one of the first states to establish a system of state-supported treatment of mental illness and disabilities, replacing local [almshouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almshouse). Dix came into this effort after having met J. O. King, a [Jacksonville, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonville,_Illinois) businessman, who invited her to Illinois, where he had been working to build an asylum for the insane. With the lobbying expertise of Dix, plans for the Jacksonville State Hospital (now known as the [Jacksonville Developmental Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonville_Developmental_Center)) were signed into law on March 1, 1847.[^cite_note-44]&#x20;

#### Civil War and after

Main article: [Illinois in the American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_in_the_American_Civil_War)



[Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_\(American_Civil_War\)) troops embarking at [Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo,_Illinois) on January 10, 1862

During the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), Illinois ranked fourth in soldiers who served (more than 250,000) in the [Union Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Army), a figure surpassed by only New York, [Pennsylvania](23332.md), and [Ohio](22199.md). Beginning with President [Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln)'s first call for troops and continuing throughout the war, Illinois mustered 150 infantry regiments, which were numbered from the 7th to the 156th regiments. Seventeen cavalry regiments were also gathered, as well as two light artillery regiments.[^cite_note-45] The town of [Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo,_Illinois), at the southern tip of the state at the confluence of the Mississippi and Ohio Rivers, served as a strategically important supply base and training center for the [Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_\(American_Civil_War\)) army. For several months, both General [Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulysses_S._Grant) and Admiral [Foote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Hull_Foote) had headquarters in Cairo.&#x20;

During the Civil War, and more so afterwards, Chicago's population skyrocketed, which increased its prominence. The [Pullman Strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pullman_Strike) and [Haymarket Riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haymarket_affair), in particular, greatly influenced the development of the American [labor movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_movement). From Sunday, October 8, 1871, until Tuesday, October 10, 1871, the [Great Chicago Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Chicago_Fire) burned in downtown Chicago, killing 300 people and destroying four sq mi (10 km<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-46]&#x20;

### 20th century



Police and protesters at the [1968 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Democratic_National_Convention) in Chicago.



Rising waters in [Alton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alton,_Illinois) in 1993.

At the turn of the 20th century, Illinois had a population of nearly 5 million. Many people from other parts of the country were attracted to the state by employment caused by the expanding industrial base. Whites were 98% of the state's population.[^cite_note-census-47] Bolstered by continued [immigration from southern and eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_immigration_to_the_United_States), and by the African-American [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)) from the South, Illinois grew and emerged as one of the most important states in the union. By the end of the century, the population had reached 12.4 million.&#x20;

The [Century of Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Century_of_Progress) [World's fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World%27s_fair) was held at Chicago in 1933. Oil strikes in [Marion County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_County,_Illinois) and [Crawford County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crawford_County,_Illinois) led to a boom in 1937, and by 1939, Illinois ranked fourth in U.S. oil production. Illinois manufactured 6.1 percent of total United States military armaments produced during [World War II](32927.md), ranking seventh among the 48 states.[^cite_note-48] Chicago became an ocean port with the opening of the [Saint Lawrence Seaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_Seaway) in 1959. The seaway and the [Illinois Waterway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Waterway) connected Chicago to both the Mississippi River and the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md). In 1960, [Ray Kroc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_Kroc) opened the first [McDonald's](2480627.md) franchise in [Des Plaines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Des_Plaines,_Illinois), which was demolished in 1984.[^cite_note-\:0-49] In 1985 a replica was built on the same site to recreate how the original one looked.[^cite_note-\:0-49] Though this replica was demolished in 2017, due to repeated flooding of the building.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51]&#x20;

Illinois had a prominent role in the emergence of the [nuclear age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_age). In 1942, as part of the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project), the [University of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Pile-1) conducted the first sustained [nuclear chain reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_chain_reaction). In 1957, [Argonne National Laboratory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argonne_National_Laboratory), near [Chicago](6886.md), activated the first experimental nuclear power generating system in the United States. By 1960, the first privately financed nuclear plant in the United States, [Dresden 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dresden_Nuclear_Power_Plant), was dedicated near [Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morris,_Illinois). In 1967, [Fermilab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermilab), a national nuclear research facility near [Batavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batavia,_Illinois), opened a [particle accelerator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_accelerator), which was the world's largest for over 40 years. With eleven plants currently operating, Illinois leads all states in the amount of electricity generated from nuclear power.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53]&#x20;

In 1961, Illinois became the first state in the nation to adopt the recommendation of the [American Law Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Law_Institute) and pass a comprehensive criminal code revision that repealed the law against [sodomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodomy_laws_in_the_United_States). The code also abrogated [common law crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law_crime) and established an [age of consent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_consent) of 18.[^cite_note-54] The state's [fourth constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Constitution) was adopted in 1970, replacing the 1870 document.[^cite_note-55]&#x20;

The first [Farm Aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farm_Aid) concert was held in [Champaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champaign,_Illinois) to benefit American farmers, in 1985. The worst [upper Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Mississippi_River) flood of the century, the [Great Flood of 1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Flood_of_1993), inundated many towns and thousands of acres of farmland.[^cite_note-Biles-30]&#x20;

### 21st century



A COVID-19 safety message in the Chicago Loop

Illinois entered the 21st century under Republican Governor [George Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Ryan). Near the end of his term in January 2003, following a string of high-profile exonerations, Ryan commuted all [death sentences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Illinois) in the state.[^cite_note-56]&#x20;

The [2002 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Illinois_gubernatorial_election) brought Democrat [Rod Blagojevich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Blagojevich) to the governor's mansion. It also brought future president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) into a committee leadership position in the Illinois Senate, where he drafted the [Health Care Justice Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_Care_Justice_Act), a forerunner of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act).[^cite_note-fight-tested-57] Obama's election to the presidency in Blagojevich's second term set off a chain of events culminating in [Blagojevich's impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Blagojevich_corruption_charges), trial, and subsequent criminal conviction and imprisonment, making Blagojevich the second consecutive Illinois governor to be convicted on federal corruption charges.[^cite_note-58]&#x20;

Blagojevich's replacement [Pat Quinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Quinn_\(politician\)) was defeated by Republican [Bruce Rauner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Rauner) in the [2014 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Illinois_gubernatorial_election). Disagreements between the governor and legislature over budgetary policy led to the [Illinois Budget Impasse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Budget_Impasse), a 793-day period stretching from 2015 to 2018 in which the state had no budget and struggled to pay its bills.[^cite_note-59]&#x20;

On August 28, 2017, Rauner signed a bill into law that prohibited state and local police from arresting anyone solely due to their immigration status or due to federal detainers.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] Some fellow Republicans criticized Rauner for his action, claiming the bill made Illinois a [sanctuary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_city).[^cite_note-62]&#x20;

In the [2018 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Illinois_gubernatorial_election), Rauner was replaced by [J. B. Pritzker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._B._Pritzker), returning the state government to a Democratic [trifecta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_trifecta).[^cite_note-63] In January 2020, the state [legalized marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Cannabis_Regulation_and_Tax_Act).[^cite_note-64] On March 9, 2020, Pritzker issued a disaster proclamation due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Illinois). He ended the state of emergency in May 2023.[^cite_note-65]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Illinois)

Further information: [List of ecoregions in Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ecoregions_in_Illinois)



[Illinois municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Illinois)

Illinois is located in the [Midwest region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States) of the United States and is one of the eight states in the [Great Lakes region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes_region_\(North_America\)) of North America. Illinois's eastern border with [Indiana](21883857.md) consists of a north–south line at 87° 31′ 30″ west longitude in [Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) at the north, to the [Wabash River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wabash_River) in the south above [Post Vincennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincennes,_Indiana). The Wabash River continues as the eastern/southeastern border with Indiana until the Wabash enters the [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River). This marks the beginning of Illinois's southern border with [Kentucky](16846.md), which runs along the northern shoreline of the Ohio River.[^cite_note-66] Most of the western border with [Missouri](19571.md) and [Iowa](26810748.md) is the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River); Kaskaskia is an [exclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclave) of Illinois, lying west of the Mississippi and reachable only from Missouri. The state's northern border with [Wisconsin](33127.md) is fixed at 42° 30′ north latitude. The northeastern border of Illinois lies in Lake Michigan, within which Illinois shares a water boundary with the state of [Michigan](18859.md), as well as Wisconsin and Indiana.[^cite_note-Nelson-27]&#x20;

### Topography



[Charles Mound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Mound), the highest natural point in Illinois at 1,235 feet (376 m) above sea level, is located in the [Driftless Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driftless_Area) in the northwestern part of the state.

Though Illinois lies entirely in the [Interior Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interior_Plains), it does have some minor variation in its elevation. In extreme northwestern Illinois, the [Driftless Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driftless_Area), a region of unglaciated and therefore higher and more rugged topography, occupies a small part of the state. [Southern Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois) includes the hilly areas around the [Shawnee National Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawnee_National_Forest).&#x20;

[Charles Mound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Mound), located in the Driftless region, has the state's highest natural elevation above sea level at 1,235 ft (376 m). Other highlands include the [Shawnee Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawnee_Hills) in the south, and there is varying topography along its rivers; the [Illinois River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_River) bisects the state northeast to southwest. The floodplain on the Mississippi River from [Alton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alton,_Illinois) to the [Kaskaskia River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaskaskia_River) is known as the [American Bottom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Bottom).&#x20;

### Geology

Main article: [Geology of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Illinois)

During the early part of the [Paleozoic Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleozoic_Era), the area that would one day become Illinois was submerged beneath a shallow sea and located near the Equator. Diverse marine life lived at this time, including [trilobites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trilobite), [brachiopods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brachiopod), and [crinoids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crinoid). Changing environmental conditions led to the formation of large [coal swamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_forest) in the [Carboniferous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carboniferous).&#x20;

Illinois was above sea level for at least part of the [Mesozoic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesozoic), but by its end was again submerged by the [Western Interior Seaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Interior_Seaway). This receded by the [Eocene Epoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eocene).&#x20;

During the [Pleistocene Epoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleistocene_Epoch), vast ice sheets covered much of Illinois, with only the [Driftless Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Driftless_Area) remaining exposed. These [glaciers](12463.md) carved the basin of [Lake Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Michigan) and left behind traces of ancient glacial lakes and [moraines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moraine).[^cite_note-67]&#x20;

### Divisions



At 279 feet (85 m) above sea level,

[^cite_note-68]

&#x20;the lowest elevation point in the state is located near [Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo,_Illinois) and the confluence of the Ohio and Mississippi Rivers.

Illinois has three major geographical divisions. [Northern Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Illinois) is dominated by the [Chicago metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area), or Chicagoland, which is the city of Chicago and its suburbs, and the adjoining exurban area into which the metropolis is expanding. As defined by the federal government, the Chicago metro area includes several counties in Illinois, [Indiana](21883857.md), and [Wisconsin](33127.md), and has a population of over 9.8 million. Chicago itself is a cosmopolitan city, densely populated, industrialized, the transportation hub of the nation, and settled by a wide variety of ethnic groups. The city of [Rockford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockford,_Illinois), Illinois's third-largest city and center of the state's fourth largest metropolitan area, sits along Interstates [39](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_39) and [90](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_90) some 75 mi (121 km) northwest of Chicago. The [Quad Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quad_Cities) region, located along the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) in northern Illinois, had a population of 381,342 in 2011.&#x20;

The midsection of Illinois is the second major division, called [Central Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Illinois). Historically [prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie), it is now mainly agricultural and known as the Heart of Illinois. It is characterized by small towns and medium–small cities. The western section (west of the Illinois River) was originally part of the [Military Tract of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Tract_of_1812) and forms the conspicuous western bulge of the state. Agriculture, particularly [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize) and [soybeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soybean), as well as educational institutions and manufacturing centers, figure prominently in Central Illinois. Cities include [Peoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria,_Illinois); [Springfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Illinois), the state capital; [Quincy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quincy,_Illinois); [Decatur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decatur,_Illinois); [Bloomington-Normal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington-Normal,_Illinois); and [Champaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champaign,_Illinois)-[Urbana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbana,_Illinois).[^cite_note-Nelson-27]&#x20;

The third division is [Southern Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois), comprising the area south of [U.S. Route 50](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_50_in_Illinois), including [Little Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Illinois), near the juncture of the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) and [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River). Southern Illinois is the site of the ancient city of [Cahokia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cahokia), as well as the site of the first state capital at [Kaskaskia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaskaskia,_Illinois), which today is separated from the rest of the state by the Mississippi River.[^cite_note-Nelson-27][^cite_note-Horsley-69] This region has a somewhat warmer winter climate, different variety of crops (including some cotton farming in the past), more rugged topography (due to the area remaining unglaciated during the [Illinoian Stage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinoian_\(stage\)), unlike most of the rest of the state), as well as small-scale oil deposits and [coal mining](208413.md). The Illinois suburbs of [St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis,_Missouri), such as [East St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_St._Louis), are located in this region, and collectively, they are known as the [Metro-East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro-East). The other somewhat significant concentration of population in Southern Illinois is the Carbondale-Marion-Herrin, Illinois Combined Statistical Area centered on [Carbondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbondale,_Illinois) and [Marion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion,_Illinois), a two-county area that is home to 123,272 residents.[^cite_note-Nelson-27] A portion of southeastern Illinois is part of the extended [Evansville, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evansville,_Indiana), Metro Area, locally referred to as the Tri-State with Indiana and Kentucky. Seven Illinois counties are in the area.&#x20;

In addition to these three, largely latitudinally defined divisions, all of the region outside the Chicago metropolitan area is often called "[downstate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downstate_Illinois)" Illinois. This term is flexible, but is generally meant to mean everything outside the influence of the Chicago area. Thus, some cities in *Northern* Illinois, such as [DeKalb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeKalb,_Illinois), which is west of Chicago, and [Rockford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockford,_Illinois)—which is actually north of Chicago—are sometimes incorrectly considered to be 'downstate'.&#x20;

### Climate

Main articles: [Climate of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Illinois) and [List of Illinois tornadoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Illinois_tornadoes)



[Köppen climate types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) of Illinois

Illinois has a climate that varies widely throughout the year. Because of its nearly 400-mile distance between its northernmost and southernmost extremes, as well as its mid-continental situation, most of Illinois has a [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) ([Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *Dfa*), with hot, humid summers and cold winters. The southern part of the state, from about [Carbondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbondale,_Illinois) southward, has a [humid subtropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) (Koppen *Cfa*), with more moderate winters. Average yearly precipitation for Illinois varies from just over 48 in (1,219 mm) at the southern tip to about 35 in (889 mm) in the northern portion of the state. Normal annual snowfall exceeds 38 in (965 mm) in the Chicago area, while the southern portion of the state normally receives less than 14 in (356 mm).[^cite_note-Climate_Maps-70] The all-time high temperature was 117 °F (47 °C), recorded on July 14, 1954, at [East St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_St._Louis,_Illinois), and the all-time low temperature was −38 °F (−39 °C), recorded on January 31, 2019, during the [January 2019 North American cold wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2019_North_American_cold_wave) at a weather station near [Mount Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Carroll,_Illinois),[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-SCEC-72] and confirmed on March 5, 2019.[^cite_note-SCECreport-73] This followed the previous record of −36 °F (−38 °C) recorded on January 5, 1999, near [Congerville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congerville,_Illinois).[^cite_note-SCECreport-73] Prior to the Mount Carroll record, a temperature of −37 °F (−38 °C) was recorded on January 15, 2009, at [Rochelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rochelle,_Illinois), but at a weather station not subjected to the same quality control as official records.[^cite_note-pjstar-lose-74][^cite_note-Weather_Underground-75]&#x20;

Illinois averages approximately 51 days of [thunderstorm](70807.md) activity a year, which ranks somewhat above average in the number of thunderstorm days for the United States. Illinois is vulnerable to tornadoes, with an average of 54 occurring annually, which puts much of the state at about five tornadoes per 10,000 sq mi (30,000 km<sup>2</sup>) annually.[^cite_note-Annual_average_number_of_tornadoes-76] Some of [Tornado Alley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tornado_Alley)'s deadliest tornadoes on record have occurred in the state. The [Tri-State Tornado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tri-State_Tornado) of 1925 killed 695 people in three states; 613 of the victims died in Illinois.[^cite_note-paddock_nws-77]&#x20;

### Urban areas

See also: [Illinois statistical areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_statistical_areas) and [List of municipalities in Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Illinois)

Chicago is the largest city in the state and the [third-most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population) in the United States, with a population of 2,746,388 in 2020. Furthermore, over 7 million residents of the [Chicago metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area) reside in Illinois. The [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) currently lists seven other cities with populations of over 100,000 within the state. This includes the Chicago [satellite towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_town) of [Aurora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurora,_Illinois), [Joliet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joliet,_Illinois), [Naperville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naperville,_Illinois), and [Elgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elgin,_Illinois), as well as the cities of [Rockford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockford,_Illinois), the most populous city in the state outside of the Chicago area; [Springfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Illinois), the state's capital; and [Peoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria,_Illinois).&#x20;

The most populated city in the state south of Springfield is [Belleville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belleville,_Illinois), with 42,000 residents. It is located in the [Metro East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_East) region of [Greater St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_St._Louis), the second-most populous urban area in Illinois with over 700,000 residents. Other major urban areas include the [Peoria metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoria,_Illinois,_metropolitan_area), [Rockford metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockford_metropolitan_area,_Illinois), [Champaign–Urbana metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champaign%E2%80%93Urbana_metropolitan_area) (home to the [University of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Illinois_Urbana-Champaign)), [Springfield metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield_metropolitan_area,_Illinois), the Illinois portion of the [Quad Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quad_Cities) area, and the [Bloomington–Normal metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomington%E2%80%93Normal).&#x20;

## Demographics

|  | It has been suggested that this section be [**split**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) out into another article titled *[Demographics of Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Illinois)*. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Illinois)) *(September 2024)* |
| --- | --- |

&#x20;The [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) found that the population of Illinois was 12,812,508 in the [2020 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), moving from the fifth-largest state to the sixth-largest state (losing out to [Pennsylvania](23332.md)). Illinois' population slightly declined in 2020 from the [2010 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_States_census) by just over 18,000 residents and the overall population was quite higher than recent census estimates.[^cite_note-86]



Illinois 2020 population density map

Illinois is the most populous state in the [Midwest region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States). Chicago, the [third-most populous city in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population), is the center of the [Chicago metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area) or Chicagoland, as this area is nicknamed. Although the Chicago metropolitan area comprises only 9% of the land area of the state, it contains 65% of the state's residents, with 21.4% of Illinois' population living in the city of Chicago itself as of 2020.[^cite_note-87] The five [most populous counties in the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Illinois), as of 2024, are [Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_County,_Illinois) (5,182,617), [DuPage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DuPage_County,_Illinois) (937,142), [Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_County,_Illinois) (718,604), [Will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_County,_Illinois) (708,583), and [Kane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kane_County,_Illinois) (520,997), all located in the Chicago metropolitan area. While the state's population has declined in recent years, according to the 2020 census, these losses do not arise from the Chicago metro area; rather the declines are from the Downstate counties.[^cite_note-VinickyWhere2020-88] As of the 2020 census, the state's geographic mean [center of population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_population) is located at 41° 18′ 43″N 88° 22 23″W in [Grundy County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grundy_County,_Illinois), about six miles northwest of [Coal City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_City,_Illinois).[^cite_note-89]&#x20;

Illinois is the most racially and ethnically diverse state in the Midwestern United States. By several metrics, including racial and ethnic background, religious affiliation, and percentage of rural and urban divide, Illinois is the most representative of the larger [demography of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demography_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-90]&#x20;
